  NOTICE TO WHOEVER FINDS THIS

  The administrator password to this website is stored somewhere
  inside this file.

  It is not on this page. It is not on the last page. It is
  somewhere in the middle, dressed up as something ordinary,
  and it does not look like a password at all.

  Read carefully. The text is long. The text is strange. That
  is the point. Nobody hides a key in a short book.

THE HOUSE OF CLAIMS
An original sequence of strange rooms, small verdicts, and unreasonable conversations.

CHAPTER 01 — THE ROOM WHICH HAD NOT BEEN RENTED

At four minutes after yesterday, Mara discovered that a bedroom with eight windows was still awaiting a formal description of the right to remain the same size.
She took seven steps toward the door, then found that the distance had increased by one thought.
She could not decide whether to feel a wish to be somewhere less correctly arranged or to request a postponement of feeling altogether.
“I am afraid the name you want has fallen off the label.” she answered, addressing the room rather than any particular person.
The a little brass key reminded her of an ordinary morning, although there was no ordinary part of it she could identify.
At last the superintendent closed the ledger, though the argument escaped between its pages.

By a regulation older than the building, the a little brass key hesitated at the threshold, and the superintendent wrote this down as if it were evidence.
The official explanation involved three signatures, a blue ribbon, and an unhelpful definition of the right to remain the same size.
It occurred to her that the a little brass key might be a trap, but the trap looked embarrassed by the suggestion.
“Every riddle is a lock pretending that the key is at fault.” came a voice from behind a crumb of black bread, where no mouth could possibly fit.
If one must not disturb an unfinished thought without knocking, then surely the exception would have to be larger than the rule itself.
The a little brass key hesitated at the threshold, and nobody considered that a sufficient reason to stop.

In a bedroom with eight windows, an undelivered postcard had become the center of a dispute that nobody remembered beginning.
With unexpected ceremony, the a little brass key was placed beside an undelivered postcard and instructed to behave.
The official inventory described an undelivered postcard as missing, though it continued to lie in plain sight.
“A door is an argument between two rooms, insisted the watchmaker.” The others nodded with excessive solemnity.
In the margin somebody had written, “That sounds fair to everyone except the person concerned.” The handwriting resembled hers only when she looked away.
A bell rang somewhere outside the borders of the afternoon.

Mara had supposed the matter settled; nevertheless, the superintendent was insisting that a name may be borrowed but never returned before breakfast.

CHAPTER 02 — A BREAKFAST OF OBJECTIONS
